9 A. Ronald Perry Wickline.

11 A. Perry.

13 A. I'm retired.

15 this point?
16 A. Occasionally.
17 Q. What type of consulting do you do?
18 A. I work part-time as a 1099 employee for a
19 recruiting company, and then sometimes occasionally
20 for other pharmaceutical companies, sometimes in
21 selection of employees, sometimes doing some field
22 training, helping them with sales skills.
23 Q. Okay. When was the last time you did any
24 field training for a pharmaceutical company?
25 A. Probably two years ago.

1 Q. Okay. What company was that?
2 A. Kale°.
3 Q. For what product?
4 A. I think it was -- I'm going to forget the

11 A. No.

1 since you left there in 2000 -- was it '13?

7 A. No, I have not.

10 A. I have not.

21 reported.

24 A. Yes.

1 A. Yes. I'm sorry.

4 A. Correct.

9 high?
10 A. Correct.
11 Q. How did you get into sales?
12 A. I quickly realized that education is a
13 little bit like a civil service job. You only got
14 paid for your years of teaching and/or your
15 educational background. It had nothing to do with
16 the merit of how good you were as a teacher, and I

20 compensation as well as the opportunity to be
21 recognized for the job that you do.
22 Q. Did you find that to be true once you got
23 into sales?
24 A. It was.
25 Q. So let's talk about your time at

1 Mallinckrodt. You said you were the vice president

3 A. I was.
4 Q. Work forward in time. You were there a

6 A. Correct.

9 A. It did. I mean, I started that job at the

12 They were going through an expansion from I think
13 150 reps to 250 reps. So I was brought in to --
14 because I had a little bit more experience in

16 reason why I think I was selected for that position.
17 During about the last six months I knew that
18 I was going to be retiring. The company was getting
19 ready to change its focus a little bit to more of a
20 hospital emphasis, and so they were interested in
21 bringing in a new VP of sales, so I helped with the
22 transition of someone who was selected to replace
23 me. She started in around September, I think, of my
24 last year.
25 And then as the -- as she was coming on

1 board and learning the job, which happened pretty
2 rapidly, then I was less involved with the business
3 and really just kind of consulted from home the last
4 three months.
5 Q. Okay. Who was your replacement?
6 A. Stacy Chick.
7 Q. Was it your decision to leave Mallinckrodt?
8 A. It was. I was 66. I had already told them
9 that I was interested in retirement. I didn't know
10 that they already had somebody pretty much lined up
11 that they wanted to replace me with, and so that was
12 a. little bit of a surprise, but I had already
13 announced that I was interested in retiring.
14 Q. Could you have stayed at Mallinckrodt if you

16 A. No. They had -- my job had been replaced,

9 Q. Why was that information important to
10 Mallinckrodt?
11 A. The information on payors was used really to
12 help understand the kinds of questions that a
13 physician might ask, such as, "Is this product
14 covered by Medicaid," or, "Is it covered by Blue
15 Cross?" And the more that third-party payors were
16 involved, the more you knew you had to have, you
17 know, information on a lot of the private payors to
18 be able to share.
19 Because, especially in the pain environment,
20 there is nothing worse for a doctor than writing a
21 prescription for a patient in pain, having them go
22 to the pharmacy and find out that either it's not
23 available or their insurance company doesn't pay for
24 that.
25 That requires, then, the pharmacy to follow

1 up with a phone call to the physician. It might
2 require the physician to then see the patient again.
3 It just creates a lot of problems. And they were
4 pretty reluctant to write a product if they didn't
5 think was going to be available or compensated for.

22 the pharmaceutical the district had historically
23 brought in.

25 time at Mallinckrodt?

16 Q. What do you mean by that, that it was a

18 A. Well, it was a slow-release formulation
19 that -- I don't want to get too technical for you,
20 but it took three days, three to five days for the
21 blood levels to get to kind of a therapeutic level.
22 It wasn't an immediate release. It was very
23 slow release. It was a capsule that had a hole
24 drilled in it literally, and so the product just
25 slowly leached out of that pill over a period of

1 days and eventually got to a blood level that was
2 the therapeutic, but it took three to five days to
3 do that.
4 And so during that time, physicians were
5 instructed to supplement the product with more of an
6 immediate release product until you got to a steady
7 state blood level.
8 So it was a little bit more tricky than what
9 physicians were used to doing. They had to follow
10 the patients a little more carefully and make sure
11 that they saw them or talked to them and explained
12 what was going to happen so that they could
13 anticipate the fact that they could experience pain,
